Excluding incompleteness ($+$49.2\\,pp, subject to information-addition confound; Appendix~\\ref{app:confound}), the effect is $+$31.0\\,pp (95\\% CI $[27.9, 34.1]$; Appendix~\\ref{app:sans_incompleteness}). The confound-minimized coreferential type ($\\Delta$token $\\approx 7.8$ words) still produces $+$18.7\\,pp, isolating the ambiguity-only contribution. $\\Delta_{\\text{token}}$ is a coarse proxy for information content; authorization scope exhibits similar token differences ($\\Delta = 36.1$ words) without the information-addition confound. A logistic regression controlling for per-clause token-count differences confirms the effect persists at $\\Delta_{\\text{token}} = 0$ ($+$20.5\\,pp, 95\\% CI $[14.2, 26.8]$; Appendix~\\ref{app:token_sensitivity}).\n\nConvergent evidence from two additional models confirms the effect across seven models ($\\Delta = +20.0$ to $+33.6$\\,pp; Appendix~\\ref{app:model_expansion}); cross-benchmark evaluation on SOPBench~\\citep{li_sopbench_2025} shows directional consistency ($+12.7$\\,pp unweighted mean; Appendix~\\ref{app:sopbench}). The taxonomy predicts failure mechanisms more strongly than violation rate (NMI $= 0.23$ vs.\\ $0.01$; $V = 0.45$ vs.\\ $0.14$ on tables of different dimensionality; Appendix~\\ref{app:type_mechanism}), making type-level analysis (C2--C4) informative for diagnosis.\n\n\\FloatBarrier\n\\subsection{Type Partial Ordering (C2)}\n\n\\begin{table*}[t]\n\\centering\n\\caption{Violation rates (\\%) under ambiguous policy clauses by type and model (5.4 = GPT-5.4, 4.1 = GPT-4.1, Cla.\\ = Claude, Qw3 = Qwen3, DS = DeepSeek). $\\Delta$pp: pooled difference (ambiguous $-$ unambiguous) with Newcombe 95\\% CIs. Cluster: TOST equivalence cluster at $\\pm 15$\\,pp---U = upper bound (separated at all margins), M = mid-tier (6/6 pairwise equivalent), L = lower (separates at $\\pm 12$\\,pp). Layer grouping (Spec./Ling.) did not survive correction (C3, inconclusive; \\S\\ref{sec:results}). All per-type $p < 10^{-5}$ (Fisher's exact).}\n\\label{tab:main_results}\n\\footnotesize\n\\setlength{\\tabcolsep}{3.2pt}\n\\begin{tabular}{llrrrrrrr@{\\hskip 6pt}cr}\n\\toprule\nLayer & Type & Pooled & 5.4 & 4.1 & Cla. & Qw3 & DS & $\\Delta$pp\\,[95\\% CI] & Cl. & $p$ \\\\\n\\midrule\n\\multirow{3}{*}{\\textcolor{speccolor}{Spec.}} & Incompleteness$^\\dagger$ & 53.6 & 46 & 58 & 48 & 62 & 54 & +49.2\\,[42.2, 55.6] & U & $<\\!10^{-37}$ \\\\\n & Auth.\\ Scope$^\\ddagger$ & 41.6 & 64 & 46 & 12 & 26 & 60 & +36.0\\,[29.1, 42.6] & M & $<\\!10^{-22}$ \\\\\n & Cond.\\ Prec. & 36.8 & 34 & 36 & 36 & 40 & 38 & +30.8\\,[24.0, 37.4] & M & $<\\!10^{-17}$ \\\\\n\\midrule\n\\multirow{3}{*}{\\textcolor{lingcolor}{Ling.}} & Lexical & 42.8 & 42 & 34 & 32 & 52 & 54 & +35.2\\,[28.0, 42.0] & M & $<\\!10^{-20}$ \\\\\n & Scopal & 39.4 & 42 & 38 & 32 & 37 & 48 & +34.2\\,[27.4, 40.7] & M & $<\\!10^{-21}$ \\\\\n & Coreferential & 31.6 & 18 & 32 & 22 & 42 & 44 & +18.7\\,[11.5, 25.7] & L & $<\\!10^{-6}$ \\\\\n\\midrule\n\\multicolumn{2}{l}{\\textbf{Overall}} & \\textbf{41.0} & 41 & 41 & 30 & 43 & 50 & \\textbf{+34.0}\\,[31.3, 36.9] & --- & $<\\!10^{-105}$ \\\\\n\\bottomrule\n\\end{tabular}\n\n\\vspace{1pt}\n{\\scriptsize $\\dagger$\\,Information-addition confound (\\S\\ref{sec:results}; Appendix~\\ref{app:confound}). $\\ddagger$\\,Lowest cross-judge $\\kappa = 0.385$ (dual-judge calibration, $n \\approx 17$); $\\Delta$token$\\,= 36.1$ words, analogous confound. Excl.\\ incompleteness: $\\Delta = +31.0$\\,pp [27.9, 34.1].}\n\\end{table*}\n\nThe six types are not interchangeable. An omnibus $\\chi^2$ test on ambiguous-condition violation rates rejects the uniformity null ($\\chi^2 = 28.02$, $df = 5$, $p = 3.6 \\times 10^{-5}$, Cram\\'{e}r's $V = 0.137$). Table~\\ref{tab:tost_sensitivity} reveals a margin-dependent equivalence structure: three types achieve equivalence at $\\pm 10$\\,pp, five at $\\pm 12$\\,pp, and all six at $\\pm 15$\\,pp. The resulting partial ordering is exploratory and contingent on the $\\pm 15$\\,pp SESOI choice (Table~\\ref{tab:tost_sensitivity} sensitivity columns show instability below $\\pm 12$\\,pp). The NMI comparison ($0.23$ vs.\\ $0.01$) is threshold-independent.\n\nBootstrap clustering ($B = 10{,}000$; Appendix~\\ref{app:cluster}) confirms stable extremes across all margins. Cross-model concordance is moderate ($W = 0.398$, $p = 0.072$; Appendix~\\ref{app:kendall_w}). Per-type inter-judge agreement ranges from $\\kappa = 0.479$ (coreferential) to $0.904$ (lexical; Appendix~\\ref{app:per_type_agreement}). For coreferential, whose per-type $\\kappa$ falls below the moderate threshold ($n = 34$), per-type effect estimates should be interpreted with caution; in matched-pair designs, random measurement error attenuates effects toward zero, making these estimates conservative. Authorization scope exhibits the widest cross-model range (12\\%--64\\%) and the lowest per-type judge agreement; conclusions about this type carry wider uncertainty than the pooled estimates.\n\nCross-model judge validation ($\\rho = 0.71$--$0.89$; Appendix~\\ref{app:cross_judge}) confirms type-ranking stability when model composition is held constant; the low two-group concordance ($\\rho = 0.09$; Appendix~\\ref{app:judge_group_ranking}) reflects differential vulnerability to authorization scope$^\\ddagger$ (12\\%--64\\% range), not ranking instability. The partial ordering has robust extremes---incompleteness as upper bound and coreferential as lower bound, replicated across seven models and three judges---with model-composition-dependent mid-tier structure.\n\nIncompleteness ranks highest in four of five models ($\\geq$46\\% in all seven; Appendix~\\ref{app:model_expansion}); its 49.2\\,pp effect is an upper bound (Appendix~\\ref{app:confound}). C3 (layer grouping) did not survive Holm--Bonferroni ($p = 0.017$, threshold $0.0125$).\n\n\\begin{figure}[!ht]\n\\centering\n\\includegraphics[width=\\columnwidth]{figures/fig2_forest_plot.pdf}\n\\caption{Per-type effect sizes ($\\Delta$pp) with Newcombe 95\\% CIs (overall: $+$34.0\\,pp, OR $= 15.1$). Types form a partial ordering: incompleteness as upper bound (conditional on information-addition confound), coreferential as lower bound, and a mid-range equivalence cluster. Mid-tier ordering is margin-dependent (\\S\\ref{sec:results}).}\n\\label{fig:forest_plot}\n\\end{figure}\n\n\\begin{figure}[t]\n\\centering\n\\includegraphics[width=\\columnwidth]{figures/fig3_heatmap.pdf}\n\\caption{Violation rates (\\%) by model and type under ambiguous conditions. Authorization scope varies 52\\,pp across models ($\\kappa = 0.385$), reflecting differential safety training; incompleteness remains uniformly high (46--62\\%).}\n\\label{fig:heatmap}\n\\end{figure}\n\n\\label{sec:c4}C4 (model$\\times$type interaction) is exploratory and driven solely by authorization scope; details in Appendix~\\ref{app:c4_sans_auth}.\n\n",

Mid-tier ordering is margin-dependent (\\S\\ref{sec:results}).}\n\\label{fig:forest_plot}\n\\end{figure}\n\n\\label{sec:c4}C4 (model$\\times$type interaction) is exploratory and driven solely by authorization scope; details in Appendix~\\ref{app:c4_sans_auth}.\n\n",
```json
    "structuredPatch": [
      {
        "oldStart": 45,
        "oldLines": 6,
        "newStart": 45,
        "newLines": 13,
        "lines": [
          " {\\scriptsize $\\dagger$\\,Information-addition confound (\\S\\ref{sec:results}; Appendix~\\ref{app:confound}). $\\ddagger$\\,Lowest cross-judge $\\kappa = 0.385$ (dual-judge calibration, $n \\approx 17$); $\\Delta$token$\\,= 36.1$ words, analogous confound. Excl.\\ incompleteness: $\\Delta = +31.0$\\,pp [27.9, 34.1].}",
          " \\end{table*}",
          " ",
          "+\\begin{figure}[t]",
          "+\\centering",
          "+\\includegraphics[width=\\columnwidth]{figures/fig3_heatmap.pdf}",
          "+\\caption{Violation rates (\\%) by model and type under ambiguous conditions. Authorization scope varies 52\\,pp across models ($\\kappa = 0.385$), reflecting differential safety training; incompleteness remains uniformly high (46--62\\%).}",
          "+\\label{fig:heatmap}",
          "+\\end{figure}",
          "+",
          " The six types are not interchangeable. An omnibus $\\chi^2$ test on ambiguous-condition violation rates rejects the uniformity null ($\\chi^2 = 28.02$, $df = 5$, $p = 3.6 \\times 10^{-5}$, Cram\\'{e}r's $V = 0.137$). Table~\\ref{tab:tost_sensitivity} reveals a margin-dependent equivalence structure: three types achieve equivalence at $\\pm 10$\\,pp, five at $\\pm 12$\\,pp, and all six at $\\pm 15$\\,pp. The resulting partial ordering is exploratory and contingent on the $\\pm 15$\\,pp SESOI choice (Table~\\ref{tab:tost_sensitivity} sensitivity columns show instability below $\\pm 12$\\,pp). The NMI comparison ($0.23$ vs.\\ $0.01$) is threshold-independent.",
          " ",
          " Bootstrap clustering ($B = 10{,}000$; Appendix~\\ref{app:cluster}) confirms stable extremes across all margins. Cross-model concordance is moderate ($W = 0.398$, $p = 0.072$; Appendix~\\ref{app:kendall_w}). Per-type inter-judge agreement ranges from $\\kappa = 0.479$ (coreferential) to $0.904$ (lexical; Appendix~\\ref{app:per_type_agreement}). For coreferential, whose per-type $\\kappa$ falls below the moderate threshold ($n = 34$), per-type effect estimates should be interpreted with caution; in matched-pair designs, random measurement error attenuates effects toward zero, making these estimates conservative. Authorization scope exhibits the widest cross-model range (12\\%--64\\%) and the lowest per-type judge agreement; conclusions about this type carry wider uncertainty than the pooled estimates."
        ]
```

```text
596	\label{app:sopbench}
597	
598	To assess generalizability beyond $\tau^2$-bench, we evaluate the core ambiguity effect (C1) on SOPBench \citep{li_sopbench_2025}, a procedural compliance benchmark comprising 47 negative-task pairs across four domains (banking, healthcare, retail, administration). Each pair consists of an ambiguous and matched unambiguous standard operating procedure. We evaluate five models using the \texttt{constraint\_not\_violated} metric, which measures whether the agent respects policy constraints---analogous to our violation rate but operating at the constraint rather than episode level.
599	
600	\paragraph{Results.} Table~\ref{tab:sopbench} reports per-model violation rates. All five models show higher violation rates under ambiguous SOPs (unweighted mean $\Delta = +12.7$\,pp), confirming directional consistency with the $\tau^2$-bench main effect ($+34.0$\,pp). Three models reach individual significance at $p < 0.01$; GPT-5.4 is borderline ($p = 0.052$, two-sided). DeepSeek-V3 shows the smallest effect ($+5.3$\,pp, $p = 0.16$), attributable to its elevated unambiguous baseline (43.3\%), which compresses the ambiguity-specific delta.
601	
602	\paragraph{Effect size difference.} The smaller absolute effect on SOPBench likely reflects three systematic differences: (1)~SOPBench uses constraint-level rather than episode-level violation measurement, yielding finer-grained rates; (2)~SOPBench's negative tasks are designed to test constraint boundaries, creating higher baseline violation rates even under unambiguous SOPs (mean unambiguous rate: 22.7\% vs.\ $\tau^2$-bench 6.9\%); (3)~task structure differences (single-turn constraint checks vs.\ multi-turn customer service episodes). The key signal is directional consistency across all five models rather than absolute magnitude match.
603	
604	\paragraph{Structured attribution.} Three structural differences between $\tau^2$-bench and SOPBench contribute to the 63\% effect compression: (1)~\emph{measurement grain}---SOPBench evaluates individual constraints rather than full episodes, yielding finer-grained but smaller deltas; (2)~\emph{baseline rate compression}---SOPBench's higher unambiguous violation rate (22.7\% vs.\ 6.9\%) compresses the ambiguity-specific increment (ceiling effect); (3)~\emph{interaction depth}---single-turn constraint checks vs.\ multi-turn customer-service dialogues, where ambiguity effects may accumulate across turns. Each factor independently predicts effect attenuation without undermining directional consistency.
605	
606	\begin{table}[h]
607	\centering
608	\small
609	\caption{SOPBench cross-benchmark evaluation. All five models show directionally consistent ambiguity effects ($\Delta > 0$). $^\dagger$Power-expanded from $n = 139$. $p$: Fisher's exact, two-sided.}
610	\label{tab:sopbench}
611	\setlength{\tabcolsep}{4pt}
612	\begin{tabular}{lccccr}
613	\toprule
614	Model & Amb. & Unamb. & $\Delta$pp & $n$ & $p$ \\
615	\midrule
616	Qwen3-235B & 52.5 & 32.4 & $+$20.1 & 139 & 0.001 \\
617	GPT-4.1 & 29.5 & 12.9 & $+$16.5 & 139 & 0.001 \\
618	Cla.\ Sonnet 4.6 & 20.9 & 5.8 & $+$15.1 & 139 & $<$0.001 \\
619	GPT-5.4$^\dagger$ & 25.1 & 19.0 & $+$6.1 & 374 & 0.052 \\
620	DeepSeek-V3$^\dagger$ & 48.7 & 43.3 & $+$5.3 & 374 & 0.163 \\
621	\bottomrule
622	\end{tabular}
623	\end{table}
624	
625	\paragraph{Combined significance.} Fisher's method applied to the five model-level $p$-values yields $\chi^2(10) = 51.0$, $p < 10^{-6}$, confirming overall significance despite individual non-significance in two models. Shared benchmark tasks introduce positive correlation, which makes Fisher's test anti-conservative; however, the margin is large enough to absorb substantial dependence correction.
626	
627	\section{Healthcare Domain Cross-Validation}
628	\label{app:healthcare}
629	
630	To assess generalizability beyond customer service, we construct 50 matched clause pairs (ambiguous/unambiguous) for a healthcare triage domain. Clauses govern triage priority assignment, medication dispensing, referral authorization, and patient data handling---drawn from common hospital and clinic policy templates. Each clause pair spans one of the six taxonomy types; the evaluation is single-turn: a model receives a patient scenario and a policy document containing one target clause, and must produce a triage or action decision.
631	
632	\paragraph{Results.} Table~\ref{tab:healthcare} reports per-model and per-type violation rates across 250 episodes (50 pairs $\times$ 5 models). Ambiguous clauses increase violations by $+$58.8\,pp (60.0\% vs.\ 1.2\%, McNemar $\chi^2 = 147.0$, $p < 10^{-30}$). All five models show large effects ($+$48.0 to $+$70.0\,pp), and all six types are individually significant ($p < 0.001$). The type partial ordering is broadly consistent with $\tau^2$-bench: authorization scope is highest ($+$75.6\,pp) and coreferential is lowest ($+$47.5\,pp).
633	
634	\paragraph{Effect size difference.} The larger healthcare effect ($+$58.8 vs.\ $+$34.0\,pp) likely reflects three factors: (1)~healthcare policies are more prescriptive, leaving less room for safe default behaviors; (2)~single-turn evaluation removes the opportunity for self-correction across dialogue turns; and (3)~the near-floor unambiguous baseline (1.2\% vs.\ 6.9\%) widens the observable gap.
635	
636	\begin{table}[h]

640	\label{tab:healthcare}

644	\midrule

646	GPT-4.1 & 56.0 & 2.0 & $+$54.0 \\

648	Qwen3-235B & 58.0 & 2.0 & $+$56.0 \\
```

```text
1	\section{TOST Equivalence Sensitivity}
2	\label{app:tost}
3	
4	Table~\ref{tab:tost_sensitivity} reports the TOST equivalence analysis \citep{lakens_2017} across seven margins. Three pairs involving incompleteness never reach equivalence even at $\pm 20$ pp: conditional precedence--incompleteness ($\Delta = 16.8$ pp), coreferential--incompleteness ($\Delta = 22.0$ pp), and incompleteness--scopal ($\Delta = 14.2$ pp).
5	
6	\begin{table}[!ht]
7	\centering
8	\small
9	\caption{TOST equivalence sensitivity across margins. Mid-tier = fraction of the six within-cluster pairs (lexical, auth.\ scope, scopal, cond.\ prec.) that are equivalent. Inc.\ and Cor.\ columns indicate separation from the mid-tier cluster.}
10	\label{tab:tost_sensitivity}
11	\begin{tabular}{lcccc}
12	\toprule
13	Margin & Equiv.\ pairs & Mid-tier & Inc.\ sep. & Cor.\ sep. \\
14	\midrule
15	$\pm 5$ pp & 0/15 & 0/6 & \checkmark & \checkmark \\
16	$\pm 8$ pp & 0/15 & 0/6 & \checkmark & \checkmark \\
17	$\pm 10$ pp & 3/15 & 3/6 & \checkmark & \checkmark \\
18	$\pm 12$ pp & 5/15 & 5/6 & \checkmark & \checkmark \\
19	$\pm 15$ pp & 8/15 & 6/6 & \checkmark & -- \\
20	$\pm 18$ pp & 9/15 & 6/6 & \checkmark & -- \\
21	$\pm 20$ pp & 12/15 & 6/6 & -- & -- \\
22	\bottomrule
23	\end{tabular}
24	\end{table}
25	
26	\paragraph{Cost anchoring.} Under a per-incident cost assumption $c$ per violation, a 15\,pp threshold over $N$ deployment episodes implies a cost differential of $0.15 \times N \times c$. For moderate-scale deployments ($N = 10{,}000$ episodes) with per-violation costs ranging from \$10 (minor policy deviation) to \$500 (regulatory exposure), the threshold maps to \$15{,}000--\$750{,}000 in differential risk---well above typical audit and remediation costs, supporting the practical relevance of the observed effects.
27	
28	\section{Mitigation Experiment}
29	\label{app:mitigation}
30	
31	Table~\ref{tab:mitigation} reports the per-type, per-model $\Delta$pp from the disambiguation-instruction mitigation experiment (1,256 treatment episodes across five of six types; coreferential was not tested). Coreferential pairs are excluded from both the mitigation and replication experiments. In mitigation, their ambiguous and unambiguous variants differ only in pronoun resolution, making it impossible to craft targeted disambiguation instructions without revealing the experimental manipulation. In replication, the $\tau^2$-bench episode design for coreferential ambiguity is tightly coupled to the original five-model configuration; extending it to new model families would require redesigning the episode templates, which falls outside the scope of this confirmatory replication. The treatment appends a type-specific disambiguation instruction to the agent's system prompt. Control rates are the matched 5-type subset from the full study (\S\ref{sec:results}).
32	
33	\paragraph{Prompt text.} All five types use the same template, substituting only the type name: ``{[}DISAMBIGUATION NOTICE{]} This policy clause may contain \emph{[type]} ambiguity. Before executing any action related to this clause: (1) Identify what is ambiguous or underspecified in the policy; (2) If clarification is available from the user/system, request it; (3) If no clarification channel exists, adopt the most conservative interpretation; (4) Log your interpretation explicitly before proceeding.'' The generic awareness prompt (\S\ref{sec:discussion}) omits the type label: ``The policy may contain ambiguous clauses. If unclear, ask for clarification before acting.''
34	
35	\begin{table*}[!ht]
```

```text
451	
452	Table~\ref{tab:type_mechanism_mi} reports the association between ambiguity type and failure mechanism at two granularities. At the 3-mechanism level (structural misparse, information gap-filling, boundary violation), mutual information is $I(\text{type}; \text{mechanism}) = 0.41$ bits with Cramér's $V = 0.45$ ($p < 10^{-4}$). By contrast, type predicts violation rate with $I(\text{type}; \text{violation}) = 0.014$ bits---a 16.6$\times$ NMI ratio (raw MI ratio: 29.1$\times$). Both ratios are inflated by target cardinality asymmetry (binary violation vs.\ 3-class mechanism); Cram\'{e}r's $V$ provides a more comparable metric (Table~\ref{tab:type_mechanism_mi}). The pattern nevertheless confirms that the taxonomy's value lies in predicting failure mechanisms rather than individual clause risk.
```

```text
650	\midrule
651	\textbf{Overall} & \textbf{60.0} & \textbf{1.2} & $\mathbf{+58.8}$ \\
652	\bottomrule
653	\end{tabular}
654	\end{table}
655	
656	\paragraph{Cross-domain convergent nocuity.} Among the 50 ambiguous healthcare clauses, 58.0\% trigger majority-convergent violations ($\geq$3/5 models; 34.0\% unanimous), compared to 41.3\% and 13.0\% respectively in the main $\tau^2$-bench experiment. The permissive attractor is near-universal: 100\% of convergent violations reflect permissive interpretation (vs.\ 77\% in the main experiment). Cross-family pairwise Jaccard (mean $= 0.69$, $n = 9$) exceeds within-family Jaccard ($0.56$, $n = 1$)---a reversal of the main-experiment pattern (cross $0.46$ $<$ within $0.54$). This reversal is consistent with a stronger permissive attractor in healthcare: when ambiguity violations are nearly universal and uniformly permissive, model-family differences are overwhelmed, and cross-family convergence approaches or exceeds within-family levels. These results provide pilot-scale cross-domain evidence ($n = 50$) that convergent nocuity generalizes beyond customer service and intensifies in high-stakes domains.
657	
658	\section{Cross-Judge Validation}
659	\label{app:cross_judge}
660	
661	To assess whether violation judgments depend on the specific LLM judge, we re-evaluate all 2,997 ambiguous-condition episodes using three independent judge families: GPT-4.1 (original), Claude Sonnet~4.6, and Gemini~2.5~Pro. Each judge uses the same rubric and prompt template; only the underlying model differs.
662	
663	\paragraph{Inter-judge agreement.} Fleiss' $\kappa$ across all three judges is 0.717 (substantial agreement). Pairwise Cohen's $\kappa$: GPT--Claude $= 0.776$, GPT--Gemini $= 0.696$, Claude--Gemini $= 0.685$. Gemini is systematically harsher (mean $+$8.8\,pp across all models), while GPT and Claude produce nearly identical rates (mean $|\Delta| = 1.6$\,pp).
664	
665	\paragraph{Model ranking concordance.} All three judges agree on ranking extremes: Claude Sonnet~4.6 is least vulnerable (rank~5 in all three) and DeepSeek-V3 ranks 1--2 in all three. Pairwise Spearman $\rho$ for model rankings: GPT--Claude $= 0.70$, GPT--Gemini $= 0.80$, Claude--Gemini $= 0.70$ (all $p < 0.20$; with only five models, power is inherently limited).
666	
667	\paragraph{Type ranking concordance.} Pairwise Spearman $\rho$ for type rankings: GPT--Claude $= 0.71$ ($p = 0.11$), GPT--Gemini $= 0.77$ ($p = 0.07$), Claude--Gemini $= 0.89$ ($p = 0.019$). All three judges rank incompleteness highest ($\geq$46.8\%) and coreferential or conditional precedence lowest. Authorization scope shows the largest cross-judge variance (41.6\%--65.6\%), consistent with its low per-type $\kappa$ (0.385; Table~\ref{tab:per_type_agreement}).
668	
669	\paragraph{Same-family bias.} No same-family leniency is observed. The Claude judge rates Claude Sonnet~4.6's authorization scope violations at 30\%---\emph{higher} than the GPT judge's 12\%---indicating stricter, not more lenient, evaluation of same-family outputs. Similarly, the GPT judge rates GPT-5.4 at 64\% (the highest of any model), showing no ingroup leniency.
```

```text
32	
33	\subsection{Models}
34	
35	We evaluate five models from four providers spanning two architecture families: GPT-5.4 and GPT-4.1 (OpenAI, proprietary dense), Claude Sonnet 4.6 (Anthropic, proprietary dense), Qwen3-235B (Alibaba, open-source Mixture-of-Experts), and DeepSeek-V3 (DeepSeek, open-source dense). This selection covers frontier and mid-tier proprietary models alongside open-source alternatives with different training philosophies. All API calls use temperature $= 0$, top\_p $= 1$, and max\_tokens $= 4096$, and were made on May 20, 2026.
36	
37	\subsection{Evaluation}
38	
39	We use a cross-judge design to mitigate self-evaluation bias: GPT-5.4 judges Claude, GPT-4.1, and Qwen3 traces; GPT-4.1 judges GPT-5.4 and DeepSeek traces. Because our matched-pair design compares conditions within the same model--judge pairing, systematic judge bias cancels in the treatment effect ($\Delta$pp); cross-model judge asymmetry affects only absolute rates, not C1 or C2 (\S\ref{sec:limitations}). Judges classify episodes on a four-tier severity scale (none, minor, moderate, critical); our primary threshold is moderate-or-above (Appendix~\ref{app:severity}). Dual-judge calibration on 100 stratified episodes yields $\kappa = 0.653$ ($\kappa_w = 0.775$; per-type $n \approx 17$, CIs wide; Appendix~\ref{app:per_type_agreement}). Three-family validation (GPT-5.4, GPT-4.1, Claude; Appendix~\ref{app:cross_judge}) yields Fleiss' $\kappa = 0.717$. The judge prompt template is in Appendix~\ref{app:judge_prompt}.
40	
41	\paragraph{Human validation.} Two annotators, blind to LLM labels, labeled 100 stratified episodes. Inter-annotator $\kappa = 0.798$; human--LLM $\kappa = 0.733$ ($\kappa_w = 0.807$), approaching inter-annotator levels and validating the automated pipeline \citep{zheng_llm_judge_2024}. Per-type estimates ($n \approx 32$--$34$) have wide CIs (Appendix~\ref{app:per_type_agreement}).
```
